User manual

Apache Camel 4.x Upgrade Guide

This document is for helping you upgrade your Apache Camel application from Camel 4.x to 4.y. For example, if you are upgrading Camel 4.0 to 4.2, then you should follow the guides from both 4.0 to 4.1 and 4.1 to 4.2.

The Camel Upgrade Recipes project provides automated assistance for some common migration tasks. Note that manual migration is still required. See the documentation page for details.

Upgrading Camel 4.23 to 4.24

camel-observability-services - promoted to Stable

The camel-observability-services component has been promoted from Preview to Stable support level.

KeyValueRepository and State Store - namespace partitioning on shared backends

In Camel 4.23, state-store endpoints stored keys directly into the underlying KeyValueRepository without namespacing. When a single KeyValueRepository was shared between multiple state stores, the Cache EIP, or repository adapters (such as KeyValueAggregationRepository or KeyValueIdempotentRepository), operations on one store (such as clear()) or key collisions could affect entries of another component or pattern.

Starting in 4.24:

  • StateStoreComponent automatically namespaces keys on shared backend repositories (auto-discovered from the registry or referenced via backend=#ref) under statestore:<storeName>:<key>. Delimiter characters in store names are escaped to guarantee that store names sharing a prefix remain strictly isolated. Operations such as clear(), keys(), and size() are scoped to the store’s namespace.

  • Dedicated backends created by StateStoreBackendFactory or the default per-store in-memory fallback are not namespaced.

  • To maintain backward compatibility with un-namespaced entries written by Camel 4.23 on a shared backend without migrating keys, users can opt out of namespacing by configuring namespaced=false on the endpoint (state-store:myStore?namespaced=false) or on the component (camel.component.state-store.namespaced=false).

  • The Cache EIP stores keys directly without a namespace prefix, preserving documented cache invalidation recipes (cache.delete(key) and cache.clear()). Note that because cache keys are un-prefixed, calling clear() on a KeyValueRepository shared with the Cache EIP will clear all entries in that repository, including state-store entries. It is recommended to use a dedicated repository for the Cache EIP when co-located with state-store.

  • KeyValueAggregationRepository safely verifies entry types before unmarshalling, ignoring unexpected types gracefully instead of failing with a class cast exception.

camel-ehcache - shared CacheManager and changed defaults

The following user-visible changes take effect in 4.24:

  • Shared CacheManager. Endpoints that have the same cacheName and no explicit cacheManager, cacheManagerConfiguration, or configurationUri now share one CacheManager per component instance. Previously each endpoint created its own unmanaged (UserManagedCache) cache.

    • The same name now means the same cache. ehcache:foo?action=PUT and ehcache:foo?action=GET now read and write the same entries.

    • All endpoints for a cache name must use compatible key and value types. Ehcache validates them exactly, so ehcache:foo and ehcache:foo?valueType=java.lang.String now fail at startup with a type-mismatch error.

    • The shared caches, and their entries, are closed when the last endpoint that uses the shared CacheManager stops (for example when the CamelContext stops).

  • Event delivery on default caches. A consumer on a cache without an explicit configuration now receives events. Previously, the UserManagedCache fallback lacked event executor services, so listeners were silently never called.

  • Null body rejected. PUT, PUT_IF_ABSENT and REPLACE without a body and without a CamelEhcacheValue header now fail with CamelExchangeException instead of silently storing the Message object.

  • User-supplied cacheManager caches persist. Caches created on demand inside a user-supplied cacheManager (e.g. ehcache:foo?cacheManager=#myManager) are no longer removed when Camel stops; the lifecycle of the caches follows the lifecycle of the supplied manager.

camel-flowable - xml inbound channels parse the event with the Camel XML settings

The flowable producer now parses the event of an inbound channel of type camel with deserializerType set to xml with the XML parser settings that Camel uses, which are also the settings of Flowable 8: external DTDs are not loaded, external general and parameter entities are not included, entity references are kept as entity reference nodes, and XInclude is not processed. Before, the Flowable 7 deserializer parsed the event with the JDK defaults. An event that relies on an external DTD or an external entity no longer gets their content. A channel that sets deserializerDelegateExpression keeps its own deserializer, which can parse such events.

camel-jpa - query parameters from CamelJpaParameters header are bound literally

In previous versions, string values supplied in the CamelJpaParameters header map were evaluated as Simple expressions by the JPA producer.

Starting in 4.24, parameter values provided via the CamelJpaParameters header are used as-is and bound literally as query parameters without expression evaluation. The endpoint URI option parameters continues to support Simple expression evaluation for route-configured parameters.

Routes that previously relied on evaluating Simple expressions within the CamelJpaParameters header should evaluate those expressions in the route before setting the header value.

camel-mcp-server - error replies on /mcp are JSON-RPC error responses

With the embedded HTTP server (Camel Main and Camel JBang), a request on /mcp that fails before it reaches the MCP session (wrong Content-Type, a message that cannot be parsed, a missing or unknown Mcp-Session-Id) is answered with the same HTTP status as before, and a body that is a JSON-RPC error response without an id: {"jsonrpc":"2.0","error":{"code":-32600,"message":"Session not found"}}. The messages are short and fixed. The error code is -32700 (parse error) for a message that cannot be parsed, and -32600 (invalid request) otherwise; before, a missing header or session id gave -32601 and an unknown session -32603.

camel-micrometer, camel-opentelemetry-metrics - tags and attributes from headers are used as given

The micrometer and opentelemetry-metrics producers now use the tags of the CamelMetricsTags header and the attributes of the CamelMetricsAttributes header as given. Before, their keys and values were evaluated as Simple expressions, in the same way as the tags and attributes URI options. The URI options are still evaluated per exchange, and a tag or attribute from the header still overrides one from the URI with the same key.

A route that relied on a Simple expression in a header tag or attribute should use the tags / attributes URI option instead (for example tags.customer=${header.customerId}), or compute the value in the route before setting the header.

camel-a2a - consumer context is separate from producer headers

An A2A consumer no longer populates producer control headers with the incoming operation and identity. Update consumer routes that read these headers:

Previous consumer header Replacement consumer header / exchange property

CamelA2AOperation

CamelA2ARequestOperation

CamelA2ATaskId

CamelA2ARequestTaskId

CamelA2AContextId

CamelA2ARequestContextId

To control a consumer’s SendMessage reply, replace writes to CamelA2ATaskState with CamelA2AReplyTaskState, and writes to CamelA2AResponseType with CamelA2AReplyType. The old names continue to control producers or describe their responses. Explicit producer operation headers still override the endpoint’s operation option.

Java code using A2AConstants must also migrate even though the existing constant names and values remain. For consumer metadata reads, replace OPERATION, TASK_ID and CONTEXT_ID with REQUEST_OPERATION, REQUEST_TASK_ID and REQUEST_CONTEXT_ID, respectively. For consumer reply controls, replace TASK_STATE and RESPONSE_TYPE with REPLY_TASK_STATE and REPLY_TYPE.

No Endpoint DSL header helper methods were removed. The new a2ARequestOperation(), a2ARequestTaskId() and a2ARequestContextId() methods provide the consumer metadata header names; a2AReplyTaskState() and a2AReplyType() provide the consumer reply control header names. Existing helpers retain their producer roles.

Downstream results no longer replace the outer task ID, context, state or reply type, and progress updates remain attached to the outer task. Consumer reply messages use the outer context and task identity. With dataFormat=POJO, forwarding the same incoming Message instance to another agent no longer carries the outer task/context IDs. A new message supplied by the route retains its IDs, including an equal copy made with Message.builder(incoming).build(). When copying or transforming the incoming message to start a new downstream conversation, clear inherited IDs with .taskId(null).contextId(null). Set explicit downstream IDs when continuing a downstream conversation.

camel-shiro - ShiroSecurityPolicy scopes SecurityManager to policy instance

The ShiroSecurityPolicy constructors that load configuration from an INI file or object no longer set the resulting SecurityManager as Shiro’s global security manager via SecurityUtils.setSecurityManager(…​). ShiroSecurityProcessor now creates and authenticates the caller identity (principal) using the route policy’s SecurityManager directly. Applications configuring multiple ShiroSecurityPolicy instances in the same JVM now have their realms evaluated independently per policy.

Code that accesses the ambient security context or calls SecurityUtils.getSecurityManager() outside the policy and previously relied on the policy constructor setting the global security manager will now receive UnavailableSecurityManagerException. Such applications should explicitly configure SecurityUtils.setSecurityManager(policy.getSecurityManager()) at startup.

Additionally: - A ShiroSecurityPolicy created with the no-arg constructor must be configured with setSecurityManager(…​). - When alwaysReauthenticate=true (the default), no caller identity stays bound to the processing thread after the policy check completes.

camel-ai-tool - required arguments with reserved names reject the call

Required tool arguments whose names start with camel or org.apache.camel. (case-insensitive) now reject the invocation before the tool route runs. Previously, these arguments could pass the required-argument check and then be discarded by the reserved-name filter, leaving the route without a required value. Rename these parameters and update their parameter.* or argSchema declarations.

camel-mcp-server - the Origin header is checked on /mcp

On Camel Main and Camel JBang, the streamable HTTP MCP endpoint now checks the Origin header on every request (POST, the SSE GET and DELETE), as the MCP streamable HTTP transport requires. A request without an Origin header is served as before; MCP clients that run as a process send none. A request with an Origin header is served only when the origin is allowed, and gets HTTP 403 otherwise.

By default only loopback origins are allowed (localhost, 127.0.0.1 and [::1], on any port). A browser-based client served from another origin must now be listed in the new camel.server.mcp-allowed-origins option, for example camel.server.mcp-allowed-origins = https://app.example.com, http://localhost:*. The list replaces the loopback default. Scheme and host are compared ignoring case and a default port may be left out; * and other entries that are not of the form scheme://host[:port] fail the startup. Stdio mode is not affected, and neither are the Spring Boot and Quarkus engines.

camel-mina-sftp - checking whether a file exists takes one lstat request

The mina-sftp component now checks whether a remote file exists (fileExist=Ignore|Fail|Move, tempFileName, tempPrefix, a producer doneFileName) with an lstat request instead of stat. readLock=changed reads the size and modification time of the file with the same request, instead of a listing of the directory. Each check is one request, whatever the number of files in the directory, and fastExistsCheck no longer makes a difference for readLock=changed: with fastExistsCheck=true, the read lock is now acquired, where before the file was never consumed. The answer can differ from before in two cases:

  • A dangling symbolic link now exists: the link itself is checked, not its target. For example, with fileExist=Fail, sending a file with the name of a dangling link now fails, where before the file was written.

  • When the server answers the request with another status than "no such file", for example because it does not permit it on the path, the component lists the directory and compares the names, where before the file counted as missing. When the server does not answer that listing either, or the connection fails, the check now fails with an error.

readLock=changed keeps comparing the size and modification time of a symbolic link itself, as the directory listing reported them. See the MINA SFTP component.

camel-mina-sftp - TCP_NODELAY is set on the connections

The mina-sftp component now sets TCP_NODELAY on its connections, as the JSch-based sftp component does. Apache MINA SSHD leaves it off by default, so a small request, such as one that reads the next part of a directory listing, could wait for the server to acknowledge the previous reply. The org.apache.sshd.config.tcp-nodelay system property no longer applies to the connections of mina-sftp.

camel-ftp - sftp checks whether a file exists with one lstat request

The sftp component now checks whether a remote file exists (fileExist=Ignore|Fail|Move, tempFileName, tempPrefix, a producer doneFileName), and reads the size and modification time of the file for readLock=changed, with a single lstat request instead of a listing of the directory. Each check is one request, whatever the number of files in the directory, and fastExistsCheck no longer makes a difference for these checks. The answer can differ from before in two cases:

  • A dangling symbolic link now exists also with fastExistsCheck=true, as it already did without that option: the link itself is checked, not its target.

  • On a server with a case-insensitive file system, such as one backed by Windows or macOS, a file whose name differs only in case now exists: before, the names of the listing were compared case-sensitively. For example, with fileExist=Fail, sending REPORT.TXT to a directory that holds report.txt now fails, where before it replaced report.txt.

When the server answers lstat with another status than "no such file", for example because it does not permit or support it on the path, the component lists the directory as before. readLock=changed keeps comparing the size and modification time of a symbolic link itself, as the directory listing reported them. See the SFTP component.

Java

Breaking change: Camel 4.24 requires Java 21 as the minimum runtime version. Java 17 is no longer supported. Upgrade your JDK to 21 or later before upgrading to Camel 4.24.

camel-core - Package scanning of JARs no longer includes sibling packages

When scanning classes in a package (for example camel.main.base-package-scan=com.acme.routes, or PackageScanClassResolver.findImplementations), classes found in a JAR were also included when they belonged to a sibling package whose name merely started with the scanned package name, such as com.acme.routesupport or com.acme.routes2. Scanning a directory on the classpath never did this. Both now only include the scanned package and its sub packages, so a RoutesBuilder or @Configuration class in such a sibling package is no longer discovered when running from a JAR; list that package explicitly if it should be scanned.

camel-core - Model has a new getModelLock method

The org.apache.camel.model.Model interface (and so ModelCamelContext) has a new method getModelLock(), which returns the lock held while routes are added to or removed from the model. A custom implementation of Model must implement it (classes that extend DefaultCamelContext need no change). The Kamelet component now creates the routes of Kamelets holding this lock.

camel-core - Route.clearRouteModel() is deprecated

The Route.clearRouteModel() method is deprecated and is now a no-op. It will be removed in a later release.

This method dropped the references a runtime route held to its model (the route definition, the error handler factory and the error handlers) once the route was created at startup. The CamelContext already keeps the route definitions in its model, so clearing them on the route saved little memory at the cost of making Route.getRoute() and Route.getErrorHandlerFactory() return null for routes created at startup (but not for routes added after startup).

Applications that called Route.clearRouteModel() directly can remove the call. Implementations of the Route interface that override clearRouteModel() can remove the override.

camel-management - LastExchangeCompletedExchangeId is updated at most once per millisecond

The JMX attribute LastExchangeCompletedExchangeId, of the CamelContext, route and processor MBeans, is now updated at most once per millisecond, so that the JMX statistics no longer generate an exchange id for every exchange. When several exchanges complete within the same millisecond, the attribute has the id of the first one, not of the last one. Failed exchanges are not affected: LastExchangeFailureExchangeId is still updated for every failed exchange.

camel-core - the activity of the backlog tracer is replaced by the timeline dev console

The activity tracking of the backlog tracer (since 4.22) is removed. It kept the last 100 completed exchanges, which under load was only the last seconds. The new timeline dev console takes its place: for each second of the last 5 minutes it records what each route did (completed, recovered by a redelivery, handled by the error handler, failed), the mean, p95 and max time, the errors by route and exception, and per second the failed, handled and recovered exchanges and the 3 slowest with the remote calls they made. It is on whenever the dev consoles are, as with camel run (profile dev), and the number of seconds is set with camel.devConsole.timeline.seconds (60 to 3600, default 300).

Removed:

  • BacklogTracer.isActivityEnabled, setActivityEnabled, getActivitySize, setActivitySize, getActivity, dumpActivity and dumpActivityAsJSon, and the interface org.apache.camel.spi.BacklogTracerActivityMessage.

  • The JMX attributes ActivityEnabled and ActivitySize, and the operation dumpActivityAsJSon, of the backlog tracer MBean.

  • The options camel.trace.activityEnabled and camel.trace.activitySize (also of Spring Boot and Quarkus).

  • The exchange property Exchange.ACTIVITY_SPAN_TAGS (CamelActivitySpanTags), which camel-telemetry set when activity tracking was on.

  • The activity dev console, which the timeline dev console replaces.

  • camel get activity, which camel get timeline --exchanges replaces. camel get timeline shows a line per route, and --errors the errors by route and exception.

  • The <pid>-activity.json file and the activity snapshot of the CLI connector, which are now <pid>-timeline.json (the last 10 seconds) and timeline. The new action timeline returns the whole timeline.

camel-management - an exchange recovered by a redelivery is counted when the route removes the Camel headers

The Redeliveries statistics of the routes, processors and the CamelContext count an exchange that completed after a redelivery also when the route removed the CamelRedelivered header afterwards (such as removeHeaders("Camel*") before calling an endpoint). Before, such a recovery was not counted. ExchangeHelper.isRedelivered now also returns true when the error handler redelivered the exchange, without looking at the header only. An exchange whose failure is continued (continued(true)) no longer keeps its redelivery counter, as with a handled failure.

camel-management - the input and reply hits of the runtime endpoint registry count each message once

The runtime endpoint registry (in extended statistics mode, as the RuntimeEndpointRegistry MBean, the endpoints dev console and the Camel CLI show it) now counts an input hit of a route when an exchange enters the route through its consumer, as the route counts its exchanges. Before, it counted every created exchange: the copy of a Circuit Breaker and the sub-exchanges of a Split, Multicast or Recipient List were counted as input hits of the route as well, and a route that consumes from direct (or got a message sent by a ProducerTemplate to its direct endpoint) had no input hits at all.

Likewise, the reply of an InOut consumer is counted as an out hit of its endpoint when the exchange is done in the route, once per message. Before, the completed copy of a Circuit Breaker and the sub-exchanges of a Split, Multicast or Recipient List were counted as replies as well.

camel-core - a step after a redelivered step gets all its redeliveries

When a step of a route was redelivered and then succeeded, a later failure (of another step of the route, or of a route that gets the exchange or a copy of it next, such as over seda) counted its redeliveries on from the CamelRedeliveryCounter header that the first step left on the message. With maximumRedeliveries(2), a step after one that needed two redeliveries was not redelivered at all: the message went to the dead letter channel at its first failure. Now such a failure is redelivered as any other, from attempt 1, so such routes can redeliver more often than before. The CamelRedelivered, CamelRedeliveryCounter and CamelRedeliveryMaxCounter headers stay on the message after the redelivered step succeeded, as before.

camel-core - a nested split no longer changes the processed items of the outer split

With errorThreshold or maxFailedRecords on a split that contains another split, the inner split changed the number of processed items of the outer split. It counted its own items when it had no thresholds, and hid the items of the outer split when it had them. SplitResult.getProcessedItems() now reports the items of that split only. A nested split with errorThreshold can now stop earlier than before, as the ratio is computed from the correct number.

camel-util - BoundedExecutorService: a task of the executor does not wait for a permit

With virtual threads, a task that runs in a thread pool bounded by maxQueueSize and submits more work to it, such as a parallel split, no longer waits for a permit when none is available. It runs the work itself at once, whatever the rejected policy. Before, it could wait for a permit it held itself, until keepAliveTime expired, or forever with Block. A split with a timeout could lose items that way. With Abort, a parallel split ran one item fewer at a time than maxQueueSize allows, and with maxQueueSize 1 it was rejected after keepAliveTime. A submitter outside the pool waits, and is rejected with Abort, as before.

camel-core - suspendRoute on a route that is not started leaves it as it is

suspendRoute on a route that is not started (stopped, or with autoStartup=false and never started), from the RouteController, JMX, the control bus, the CLI (camel cmd suspend-route) or a route policy such as DurationRoutePolicy, now leaves the route in its current state, and route policies no longer get onSuspend for it. Before, the route became Suspended, and a later startRoute or resumeRoute resumed it without starting its consumer, so it consumed nothing. Code that checks the status of a route after suspendRoute now sees Stopped for such a route. The last error of such a route, for example from a failed start, is kept.

camel-core - Routing Slip and Dynamic Router fire the sent event of a step before the next step

The ExchangeSentEvent of a step of a routingSlip or dynamicRouter is now fired when that step is done, before the next step is sent, as with recipientList. Before, when a step completed asynchronously (nearly every step, as the error handler of each step schedules its work), the sent events of the steps were fired in reverse order after the last step: the time taken of a step included all the steps after it, and the sent event of the first step carried the message and the failure after the last step.

Users of the sent events see the change: the time of a step is lower (such as in the exchange event notifier timers of camel-micrometer and camel-opentelemetry-metrics), the first step is no longer reported as failed because a later step failed, and event-based tracing (camel-jfr, and camel-telemetry together with its own fix for asynchronous steps) pairs the events of each step. There is no option to keep the old order.

camel-core - RedeliveryErrorHandler.defensiveCopyExchangeIfNeeded is deprecated

When redelivery is enabled, the error handler (DefaultErrorHandler, DeadLetterChannel and the transaction error handlers) keeps a copy of the message before each step of the route, so a redelivery starts again from the original message. It copied the whole exchange for that, at every step, also when the step succeeded, but a redelivery only ever restored the IN message from it. It now keeps a copy of the IN message only. Routes behave as before: exchange properties and variables were not restored on redelivery, and still are not.

The protected RedeliveryErrorHandler.defensiveCopyExchangeIfNeeded(Exchange) method is deprecated and no longer called. A custom error handler that overrides it to change what is redelivered must change the message before the redelivery instead, for example with onRedelivery.

camel-core - the documented default of skipBindingOnErrorCode is true

The skipBindingOnErrorCode option of the Rest DSL (on restConfiguration, rest, its verbs and restBinding) is now documented with the default value true, the default it has always had at runtime. Before, the model, the catalog, the XML and YAML DSL schemas said false. Routes behave as before.

Tools that read the default from the catalog or the schemas now see true.

camel-core - pollEnrich: the body is null after a timeout also for a message that creates its body on demand

When pollEnrich (or poll) times out and no aggregationStrategy is configured, the body is set to null, and with variableReceive the variable is null. For a message that creates its body on demand, the null did not stick: the next read created the body again. The message of a file consumer creates its body from its file, so after a timeout the body, or the variable of variableReceive, was the file of the incoming exchange instead of null. The messages of the JMS, sjms, mail, HTTP, XMPP, SMPP, SNMP and Spring WS consumers create their body on demand as well, so there it was the payload of the consumer again.

The body, or the variable, is now null after a timeout for these consumers too. A route that read the incoming file or payload from the body after a timed out pollEnrich must keep it before the pollEnrich instead, for example with variableReceive, which leaves the body as it was. To keep the body empty, the message is replaced by a plain message with the same message id, headers and attachments, so after a timeout it is no longer of the message type of the consumer (such as GenericFileMessage or JmsMessage).

camel-core - Loop EIP breakOnShutdown

The Loop EIP with breakOnShutdown now only breaks out when the route (or Camel) is stopped, and no longer on suspend. A suspend waits for the loop like any other inflight exchange. A loop that never ends on its own (such as a doWhile with breakOnShutdown) is therefore no longer broken at once by a suspend: the suspend waits until the shutdown timeout, and the forced suspend then breaks the loop and stops the consumer of the route.

camel-core - doFinally runs all its steps after a called route handled a failure

When a doTry calls a route (such as with direct) whose error handler handles a failure (a dead letter channel or a handled onException), the doFinally block now runs all its steps. Before, in all Camel 4.x releases, it ran only its first step and skipped the rest, so a second or later step of the block (such as a to, a recipient list or a routing slip) did not send its message. A route called from such a doFinally that fails is now handled (or continued) by its own error handler, as anywhere else; before, its error handler left the failure alone and the exchange failed with that exception. As before, the route does not continue after the doTry once a called route handled the failure.

camel-core - an AsyncProcessor that throws on the first attempt goes through the error handler

When the error handler of a step can redeliver, has an onException or an onPrepareFailure processor, or is a dead letter channel, and the processor of the step (such as a custom AsyncProcessor used with process(…​); an endpoint in to(…​) is not affected) throws from process(exchange, callback) on the first attempt instead of setting the exception on the exchange, the error handler now handles that failure as any other failure of the step: it redelivers it, and when the redeliveries are exhausted, the matching onException or the dead letter channel handles it. This is what it already did when the processor set the same exception on the exchange, or when a plain Processor threw it. Before, the exchange failed at once with the thrown exception. When such a processor calls its callback and then throws, the exception is now only logged at WARN; when it handed its callback to another thread before it threw, and that thread calls it after the error handler took over the failure, or when it calls its callback twice, that call is now ignored and logged at WARN. Before, the exchange was continued a second time in these cases.

To not redeliver such a failure, add an onException for its exception type with maximumRedeliveries(0); as for the same failure set on the exchange, the dead letter channel and the onException still handle it. There is no option to let such a failure skip the dead letter channel or onException as before; a plain Processor that throws gets the same handling. Better, fix the processor to set the exception on the exchange and call the callback, as an AsyncProcessor must.

camel-core - Aggregate EIP no longer completes the groups when the route is suspended

With forceCompletionOnStop or completeAllOnStop, suspending the route no longer completes the aggregated groups. They are kept and completed when the route or Camel is stopped. To send them out before a suspend, use forceCompletionOfAllGroups (AggregateController or JMX), or stop the route instead.

camel-core - Aggregate EIP does not confirm a group that was completed by its first exchange

A group that is completed by the exchange that creates it, for example with completionSize(1), is never added to the aggregation repository. With a RecoverableAggregationRepository, Camel no longer calls confirmWithResult for such a group. Before, JdbcAggregationRepository logged an ERROR for each one and kept its exchange id as unconfirmed until the route was removed. A custom RecoverableAggregationRepository should not expect confirmWithResult for these groups.

camel-core - ThrottlingExceptionRoutePolicy counts the failures within the failure window

The circuit of ThrottlingExceptionRoutePolicy now opens when failureThreshold failures occurred within the last failureWindow milliseconds, as documented. Before, it counted every failure since the circuit last closed (or since the route started), and only the last failure had to be within the window, so failures spread over hours opened the circuit on a single new failure. Routes whose failures are spread out therefore open their circuit less often now; to open it sooner, lower failureThreshold or raise failureWindow.

In the half open state, a successful exchange now closes the circuit, and a failed exchange opens it again. Before, a successful exchange opened the circuit again as long as the failures that opened it were within failureWindow; with the defaults (a window of 60 seconds, a half open check after 30 seconds) a success in the first 30 seconds after the half open check therefore kept the circuit open. A halfOpenHandler decides as before.

camel-core - the failover load balancer does not fail over on a suppressed exception

The failover load balancer with a list of exceptions now matches only the exception the attempt fails with and its causes, as onException and doCatch do. Before, it also matched the suppressed exceptions of that exception. Since 4.23, an exception thrown in a doFinally block is added as a suppressed exception to the exception from the doTry block, so a doFinally that threw one of the exceptions to fail over on made the load balancer fail over, and the exception from the doTry block was lost when the next processor succeeded. Now the exchange fails with that exception. The same applies to a failing onCompletion that runs before the consumer (modeBeforeConsumer) and a failing saga compensation, whose exceptions are also added as suppressed exceptions.

The load balancer also no longer fails over when one of the exceptions to fail over on is only the suppressed exception of the exception the attempt fails with in these cases:

  • An onException block (or another error handler) fails while it handles the exception: the exchange fails with the new exception, and the original exception is added to it as a suppressed exception (by the FatalFallbackErrorHandler). Unlike the doFinally case, this is not a 4.23 regression: it changes behaviour that dates back to Camel 2.16, intentionally, so that the load balancer matches exceptions as onException and doCatch do.

  • The error handler redelivers the attempt (with inheritErrorHandler enabled, or the error handler of the route the attempt calls) and the last redelivery fails with a different exception than the first attempt: the exchange fails with the exception of the last redelivery, and the exception of the first attempt is added to it as a suppressed exception. For example, with only java.io.IOException in the list, when the first attempt fails with a java.net.ConnectException and the redelivery with an HttpOperationFailedException, the load balancer used to fail over and now does not.

  • The onWhen predicate of a doCatch throws an exception: the exchange fails with the exception of the predicate, and the original exception is added to it as a suppressed exception.

  • A processor or component adds a suppressed exception itself (such as Java’s try-with-resources when closing a resource fails).

To fail over in these cases, add the type of the exception the attempt fails with to the list.

camel-core - ThrottlingExceptionRoutePolicy does not count a failure on a suppressed exception

The exceptions of ThrottlingExceptionRoutePolicy (its exceptions option, or the list given to its constructor) are now matched against the exception the exchange fails with and its causes only, as onException, doCatch and the failover load balancer do. Before, they also matched the suppressed exceptions of that exception, so such a failure counted towards opening the circuit. Since 4.23, an exception thrown in a doFinally block is added as a suppressed exception to the exception from the doTry block, so a doFinally that threw one of the listed exceptions counted the failure, although the exception from the doTry block was not listed. The same applies, since 4.23, when the onWhen predicate of a doCatch throws an exception: the exchange fails with the exception of the predicate, and the original exception is added to it as a suppressed exception.

A failure also no longer counts when one of the listed exceptions is only the suppressed exception of the exception the exchange fails with in other cases, for example:

  • An onException block (or another error handler) fails while it handles the exception: the exchange fails with the new exception, and the original exception is added to it as a suppressed exception (by the FatalFallbackErrorHandler).

  • The error handler redelivers and the last redelivery fails with a different exception than the first attempt: the exchange fails with the exception of the last redelivery, and the exception of the first attempt is added to it as a suppressed exception.

  • An onCompletion that runs before the consumer (modeBeforeConsumer) or a saga compensation fails, or a processor or component adds a suppressed exception itself (such as Java’s try-with-resources when closing a resource fails).

Unlike the doFinally and onWhen cases, these are not 4.23 regressions; they change older behaviour intentionally, so that the policy matches exceptions as onException and doCatch do.

To count these failures, add the type of the exception the exchange fails with to exceptions, or leave exceptions empty to count every failure.

camel-core - JsonObject and JsonArray convert to their JSON text

The camel-util-json types org.apache.camel.util.json.JsonObject and JsonArray (given by convertBodyTo: JsonObject, graphql, kubernetes-custom-resources and typesafe-ai) now have type converters to String, byte[] and InputStream that give their JSON text, using the charset of the exchange for the bytes. Before, a String was the toString of the map or list ({sku=CAMEL-MUG, qty=3}, which is not JSON), and there was no conversion to byte[] or InputStream, so such a body sent to http or a file failed. A JsonObject or JsonArray that holds a value that is not JSON, such as a POJO, converts as before.

camel-core - TypeConversionException gives the value that failed to convert

When a type converter fails during the conversion of a message, the TypeConversionException now gives the value that failed to convert from getValue(), and its type from getFromType() and in the message (Error during type conversion from type: …​). Before, they gave the value as a String prepared for logging, so the message always said from type: java.lang.String. The message does not include the value itself.

The conversion of a collection or array to an array whose component type does not fit an element, such as a list of strings to int[] or byte[], now gives no conversion (null, or NoTypeConversionAvailableException for a mandatory conversion), and other type converters can try. Before, it failed with a TypeConversionException due to IllegalArgumentException: argument type mismatch or an ArrayStoreException.

camel-core - a route called from a doCatch handles its failure after the doTry called a route that failed

When the doTry block calls a route (such as with direct) that fails without handling the failure (no onException handles it, and no dead letter channel), the error handler of a route called from the doCatch block now runs as it does anywhere else, as when the doTry failed in the same route: it redelivers, runs its onException (also one that does not handle the failure) and its dead letter channel, and logs the exhausted failure. Before, it gave the failure straight back, and the exchange failed with that route’s exception after one attempt. When that route handles its failure, the rest of the doCatch block and the steps after the doTry do not run, as for any failure that a called route handles; when it continues its failure, they run. When the doCatch block does not call a failing route, the route continues after the doTry as before.

The exchange no longer keeps the marks of the caught failure, which also changes the following:

  • the same applies to a later doTry in the same route, and to a doTry inside an onCompletion;

  • the same applies to a route called later from a split, multicast, recipientList, enrich or wireTap (their copies of the exchange carried the mark that the caught failure was not handled);

  • a doTry and doCatch inside an onException(…​).handled(true), whose doTry calls such a route, no longer makes the exchange fail with the exception the doCatch caught;

  • with bridgeErrorHandler=true on the consumer, a failure of such an exchange that is not handled (a doCatch that fails, or a later failure in the route) now goes to the route’s error handler, as an exchange with the body Error processing exchange, instead of only being logged;

  • as when the doTry failed in the same route, the error registry (camel get error) now also lists the failure that such a doCatch caught, and the JMX failuresHandled counters of the steps inside the doCatch block no longer count it.

To keep the exchange failing after one attempt as before, give the route called from the doCatch no error handler (noErrorHandler()); this applies to every caller of that route.

camel-core - Simple: more predicates inside ${ } are read as predicates

A predicate written inside the braces (CAMEL-24921, 4.23) is now also read as one when it uses the operators equals, !equals, !startsWith or !endsWith, and when its left side is a function with a quoted argument, such as ${substringAfter('ID-') == '42'}. Before, the whole text was read as one function: ${header.type equals 'gold'} answered null (a predicate that never matches) and ${uppercase('a') == 'B'} answered A (a predicate that always matches). The condition of a ternary inside the braces accepts equals and !equals as well: ${header.type equals 'gold' ? 'a' : 'b'} was refused. The same predicates written outside the braces, such as ${header.type} equals 'gold', are unchanged.

camel-core - Simple: the elvis operator inside the braces

The elvis operator can now be written inside the braces: ${header.limit ?: 10} means ${header.limit} ?: 10. In an expression this used to fail with ${ cannot accept ?:. In a predicate it used to be read as one function whose name took in the rest of the text, such as a header named limit ?: 10, which is not set: so ${header.limit ?: 10} == 10 was false, and is true now. Without a space on both sides of ?: nothing changes: ${header.limit ?:10} still reads a header named limit ?:10. The default value of a property placeholder, ${properties:key:a ?: b}, is not affected.

camel-core - Simple: iif and val return a quoted literal without its quotes

In the Simple language, ${iif(${header.foo} > 0,'positive','negative')} returns positive again, as it did before Camel 4.10.7 and 4.13.0 (CAMEL-22204, which made it return 'positive') and as the ternary operator ${header.foo > 0 ? 'positive' : 'negative'} does. ${val('Hello')} returns Hello; it returned 'Hello' since val was added in Camel 4.18.1. A route that removed the quotes itself, or that relied on them, must be adjusted. Values without quotes, functions such as ${body}, and the quotes inside the predicate of iif are not changed.

camel-core - Simple: a space after a comma is not part of a function argument

The arguments of the replace, substring, substringBefore, substringAfter, substringBetween and contains functions no longer include the white space around them, as for the collection functions since 4.23 (CAMEL-24969): ${replace('o', '0')} replaces o with 0 (it replaced it with 0), and the documented ${replace(&quot; , &apos;)} replaces double quotes with single quotes. The spaces inside quotes are kept, and an argument of only white space is kept as it is, so ${replace( ,-)} still replaces a space. A route that relied on a space next to the comma, such as ${replace(a,b )} for the text b , quotes it instead: ${replace(a,'b ')}. Only the quotes around a whole argument are removed: a quote inside an argument, as in a'b'c, is now kept (it was removed).

The prefix and the expression of the join function no longer include the white space around them either: for the body [A, B, C], ${join(&, id=)} gives id=A&id=B&id=C (it gave id=A& id=B& id=C). An unquoted separator is kept as written, as it can be white space on purpose: ${join(; )} still joins with ; ; a quoted separator is the text inside its quotes (${join('&' ,id=)} joins with &, it joined with & ; put the space inside the quotes to keep it: ${join('& ',id=)}). A prefix with a space at its start or end is quoted instead: ${join(&,' id=')}. As for the other functions, only the quotes around a whole argument of join are removed: a quote inside an argument, as in ${join(&,a'b'c)}, is now kept (it was removed), so an expression such as ${join(&,id=,${header.x.split(',')})} now works.

camel-core - Simple: an empty function argument is kept, and a comma in a nested function does not split

In the arguments of replace, substring, substringBefore, substringAfter, substringBetween, contains, join, split, sort, setHeader, setVariable, forEach, filter, listAdd, listRemove, mapAdd and mapRemove, an argument left out now stays in its place instead of letting the next argument take it: ${replace(b,,${header.s})} replaces b with nothing in the header (it replaced b with the text ${header.s}), and ${join(,id=)} joins with a comma and the prefix id= (it joined with id=). Where the function has no default for it, an argument left out is refused, so a stray comma that was ignored is now an error, such as ${contains(xyz,)}, ${substring(,2)} or ${replace(a,b,${body},)}: remove the comma. ${sort(true,)} now sorts the text true, and ${join( )} joins with a space (it joined with a comma). A comma inside a nested function, such as ${header.s.substring(1,3)}, no longer separates two arguments; a parenthesis outside a nested function is text, as before (${substringBetween(${body},(,))}). In the values of distinct, reverse, shuffle, list and map, a comma after an unquoted ( or { no longer separates two values: quote a value with a lone parenthesis. A lone quote at the end of an argument is now kept: ${replace(b,x')} replaces with x'.

camel-core - a route called from a doTry uses the error handler of its EIPs

A route that a doTry calls from its steps, or from its doCatch or doFinally blocks (with direct, seda, enrich and so on), now uses the error handler per output of its routing slip, dynamic router, multicast and recipient list EIPs, as it does when it is called from outside a doTry. Before, these EIPs left out that error handler while the caller was in its doTry: a routing slip redelivered the whole slip (so an endpoint that had already succeeded got the message again), a multicast or recipient list did not redeliver the failed output, and a dynamic router that counts its calls could lose the failure. The same applies to a route called from the steps of a circuit breaker. A redelivery now starts at the failed output, so such a route can make more delivery attempts than before.

When the called route handles failures (a dead letter channel, or an onException with handled(true)), a failed output of its multicast is now handled there (after the redeliveries of that error handler), and no longer reaches the doCatch of the caller.

To turn off the error handler per output of such an EIP again (no redelivery of its outputs, and the failure goes to the caller), put the EIP in a doTry with only a doFinally in the called route, or turn off the error handler of the called route with errorHandler(noErrorHandler()), which turns it off for the other steps of that route too. Neither option brings back the redelivery of a whole routing slip or dynamic router.

camel-core - an AsyncProcessor that throws in a step without an error handler fails the exchange

A step that has no error handler of its own (the steps of doTry, doCatch, doFinally and onException, also when nested in another EIP inside them, the outputs of a multicast, the recipients of a recipient list inside a doTry, the steps of a circuit breaker, and a step with inheritErrorHandler(false)) now handles a processor that throws from process(exchange, callback) instead of setting the exception on the exchange as if it had set it: the exchange fails with that exception, so the doCatch catches it, and the output of a multicast is redelivered by the error handler of the route as for any other failure. Before, the exchange hung.

When such a processor handed its callback to another thread and then throws, the exchange now fails with the thrown exception unless that thread calls the callback first, and a later call is ignored and logged at WARN; before, the exception was dropped and that callback continued the exchange as a success. A second call of the callback is ignored and logged at WARN; before, the exchange was continued a second time. There is no option for the old behaviour; fix the processor to set the exception on the exchange and call the callback, as an AsyncProcessor must.

camel-core - variableReceive into a route or group variable stores the header variables in that route or group

With variableReceive (toV, enrich, pollEnrich, fromV and so on) into a route variable or a group variable, the received headers are now stored in the route (group) of the variable. Before, they were stored under a route (group) with the id header, so they could only be read with an internal name, and were not removed with the route.

Variable Header variable before Header variable now

route:resp (in route rs)

route:header:rs:resp.status

route:header:resp.status or route:rs:header:resp.status

group:myGroup:resp

group:header:myGroup:resp.status

group:myGroup:header:resp.status

Inside a route, route:header:resp.status now means the header variable of the route variable resp of the current route, not the variable resp.status of a route with the id header.

camel-util - FilterIterator.remove after hasNext throws IllegalStateException

FilterIterator.hasNext() looks ahead in the iterator it wraps, so a remove() after next() and then hasNext() removed the element after the one next() returned, and a remove() before any next() removed an element as well. remove() now throws IllegalStateException unless it directly follows next(). Code that called hasNext() between next() and remove() must call remove() before hasNext().

The same applies to a remove() after a next() that failed after it looked at further elements: a NoSuchElementException because the filter rejected all the elements that were left (or they were null), or an exception thrown by the filter or by the iterator it wraps. Such a remove() could remove an element that next() never returned; it now throws IllegalStateException and removes nothing. A next() that fails at the end of the wrapped iterator, without looking at any element, still leaves the element returned before it removable.

camel-util - CaseInsensitiveMap merges keys that differ only in the case of a supplementary letter

CaseInsensitiveMap, which holds the message headers, now treats two keys that differ only in the case of a letter outside the Basic Multilingual Plane (such as U+10400 and U+10428) as the same key, as String.equalsIgnoreCase does; before, they were usually kept as two entries. Keys without such letters are not affected.

camel-core - ThrottlingExceptionRoutePolicy runs the half open check on a thread pool of Camel

The half open check of ThrottlingExceptionRoutePolicy now runs on a thread pool created through the ExecutorServiceManager (one thread, named ThrottlingExceptionRoutePolicy and managed like the other thread pools of Camel), instead of a new java.util.Timer thread each time the circuit opens. The thread is created when the circuit opens the first time, and it ends when the last route of the policy is removed or the policy is stopped.

halfOpenAfter must now be greater than 0: adding a route with a policy whose halfOpenAfter is 0 or less fails, and setHalfOpenAfter (also over JMX) rejects such a value with an IllegalArgumentException. With 0, a check that keeps the circuit open (keepOpen, or a halfOpenHandler that is not ready) scheduled the next check without delay, a busy loop. To half open the circuit almost at once, as 0 did, use a small value such as 1.

An exception thrown by the isReadyToBeClosed method of a halfOpenHandler is now logged (by the exception handler of the policy), and the circuit is kept open and checked again after halfOpenAfter; before, the timer thread died and the circuit stayed open.

camel-core - Split EIP keeps the original message when maxFailedRecords or errorThreshold stops it

When the Split EIP stops because maxFailedRecords or errorThreshold is reached, the exchange now keeps its original input message, headers, exchange properties and variables, and only gets the exception, as with stopOnException. Before, the message, headers, exchange properties and variables of the split item that reached the threshold, as the steps inside the split had changed them, were copied onto the exchange.

So a doCatch around the split, an onException, an onCompletion, a dead letter channel or the caller of the route now receive the input that was split, instead of that one split item. A route that needs to know which items failed can read the CamelSplitResult exchange property, set as before: SplitResult.getFailures() has the index and the exception of the failed items.

camel-core - Split EIP processes the items again when the watermark cannot be stored

When the resume strategy of a split with watermark tracking fails to store the watermark, the split now fails with that exception, and the next split processes the same items again. Before, with synchronous(), the split also failed, but the watermark was kept in memory anyway, so the next split skipped those items although the watermark was never stored. Without synchronous(), the exchange never completed (fixed by the same change).

A watermarkExpression that throws on an item now fails that item, so stopOnException, maxFailedRecords and errorThreshold apply as for any failed item.

camel-core - shareUnitOfWork: a part sent to seda no longer takes the on completions of the parent exchange

Since Camel 4.14 the parts of a Split, Multicast or Recipient List with shareUnitOfWork use the unit of work of the parent exchange. A part that sent to an asynchronous endpoint without waiting for a reply (such as seda with an InOnly exchange) handed over all the on completions of that unit of work to its copy, including the commit or rollback of the consumer of the parent exchange (such as moving the file, or deleting the SQS message). They then ran when that copy was done, with the outcome of that copy: a file was moved to its done folder although a later part failed the split, or a message was rolled back although the split succeeded.

Now the on completions of the parent exchange stay with the parent exchange, and run when it is done, with its outcome (as the Camel 4.14 upgrade guide describes). Such a part still hands over the on completions it added to its own exchange (this is unchanged from earlier versions), so a resource the part itself opened, such as a file it polled, is still released when its copy is done. On completions added to the unit of work directly, such as by a route onCompletion that the part reaches, now stay with the parent exchange too (before, they went with the seda copy): they are done with the shared unit of work when the split is done, as for a part that does not send to seda.

As the consumer now commits when the split is done, it can commit before the seda copies have been processed. A seda copy that still reads from the input after that must get the content first. This mainly hits Multicast and Recipient List, where each part carries the whole message body (such as the file), while the parts of a Split usually carry content that was already read. For example, when the file consumer moves or deletes the file, convert the body of a file that was not loaded yet with convertBodyTo(String.class) before sending to seda. Or wait for the copy with waitForTaskToComplete=Always on the seda endpoint, which is also what to use when the message must not be committed (moved, deleted, acknowledged) before the asynchronous processing is done: a failure of the seda route then fails the part, and so the split. The previous behaviour, where the consumer commits with the outcome of one seda copy, cannot be configured.

camel-core - Tokenize: the token is literal text unless regex is true

The token of the Tokenize language is now matched as literal text, as the regex option (default false) describes. Before, the token was always compiled as a regular expression, so a delimiter such as |, ., $ or * split every character, did not split, or failed with a PatternSyntaxException. The end token of the pair mode (endToken) is also literal text now.

A route that relies on a regular expression as token, such as \s+ or (\W+)\s*, must now set regex=true:

.split().tokenize("\\s+", true)

In the XML and YAML DSL, a token with the escapes \n, \r or \t (for example \r\n) is turned into the new line, carriage return and tab characters, so it works as before. Before, only a leading \n was turned into a new line.

camel-core - Idempotent Consumer: an exchange marked rollback only removes its message id

The Idempotent Consumer now treats an exchange marked with markRollbackOnly() or markRollbackOnlyLast() (without an exception) as failed, as the SQL consumer already does: with removeOnFailure=true (the default) its message id is removed from the idempotent repository, so a redelivered message is processed instead of being dropped as a duplicate. Before, the message id was kept.

With a consumer that does not redeliver a rollback only exchange, such as the file consumer, a later message with the same id is now processed instead of being dropped as a duplicate.

To keep the message id, set removeOnFailure=false. Note that this also keeps the message id of an exchange that failed with an exception.

camel-cyberark-vault - credentials are checked at endpoint start, and a rejected token is retried only once

A cyberark-vault endpoint now fails to start when it has no usable credentials, with a message that names the options to set: the authToken option, the username and apiKey options, or the username and password options, unless the endpoint has a conjurClient. Before, an endpoint with an apiKey and no username started, and its first message failed with a NullPointerException.

When CyberArk Conjur answers HTTP 401, the endpoint and the CyberArk Conjur Vault Property Function authenticate again and retry the call once, and only when they have an apiKey or a password to authenticate with. With only an authToken, the call now fails with the 401 response, instead of retrying with the same token until a StackOverflowError. Configure the username with an apiKey or a password, instead of an authToken, to let the component get a new token when the current one expires: when the authToken is set, the component uses only that token.

camel-seda - receive without a timeout returns when the polling consumer stops

A receive() without a timeout on a seda endpoint, such as consumerTemplate.receive("seda:foo") or a pollEnrich from seda without a timeout, waited for a message for ever, also after the CamelContext, the consumer template or the route of the pollEnrich was stopped. It now returns null (no message) within about a second once the polling consumer is stopped, as EventDrivenPollingConsumer, used by most other endpoints, does. This also happens when the consumer cache of a consumer template evicts the polling consumer (see maximumCacheSize). A message that is in the queue is still returned. A polling consumer that was never started still waits for a message, as before. A caller that loops on receive() should stop on a null result.

camel-main - a failure to start is logged

A Camel Main application that fails to start now logs why, at ERROR: Error starting Camel: …​ with the stack trace, as camel run already did. Before, the failure left main() and only the JVM printed it, on stderr (Exception in thread "main" …​), so the log file of the application did not say why it stopped. The JVM no longer prints that same failure a second time. When the application logs nothing at ERROR (such as without a logging implementation), the JVM prints it as before.

camel-report-maven-plugin:validate / configuration-validate

The catalog now accepts all camel. groups defined in camel-main-configuration-metadata.json (previously only 7 of the 36 groups were recognized, besides camel.component., camel.dataformat. and camel.language.). As a result, camel-report-maven-plugin:validate and the camel-jbang MCP camel_configuration_validate tool now validate 29 additional camel. groups (including camel.server., camel.management., camel.vault., camel.ssl.*, and others).

If your project uses Spring Boot and has options such as camel.metrics.uriTagEnabled (from a Camel starter) that are not in the camel-main schema, camel-report-maven-plugin:validate may report them as "Unknown option", which fails the build when failOnError=true. The camel_configuration_validate MCP tool with runtime=spring-boot reports such keys as checked=false instead.

Group names are now matched with dash- and case-insensitive normalisation, matching how camel-main binds keys at runtime. For example, camel.error-registry.enabled and camel.ai-observability.enabled are now accepted alongside their camel-case equivalents.

If your project uses camel-report-maven-plugin:validate with failOnError=true, a key that was silently ignored before (e.g. a misspelled option under one of the newly accepted groups) may now cause the build to fail. Review the validation output and correct any misspelled keys.

The camel_configuration_validate MCP tool response format has changed:

  • PropertyValidationInfo.accepted is renamed to checked.

  • ConfigurationValidateSummary gains a new unchecked integer field counting lines whose namespace is not recognized as a Camel property (e.g. server.port=8080 or quarkus.* keys). Before, such lines were returned with accepted=false and valid=true; they are now checked=false and are left out of summary.valid and summary.errors.

If you call camel_configuration_validate programmatically and read the JSON response, update field references from accepted to checked and handle the new summary.unchecked field.

camel-semantic - expert artifacts

TypeSafe AI Semantic applications must add org.apache.camel:camel-semantic-typesafe-ai. The camel-typesafe-ai component and TypeSafe AI language remain independently usable and no longer bring in camel-semantic. The new expert artifact includes both dependencies transitively. Existing bean declarations and imports for org.apache.camel.component.typesafeai.TypeSafeAiSemanticAdapter remain valid.

Spring Boot applications using this expert must keep camel-typesafe-ai-starter and add org.apache.camel:camel-semantic-typesafe-ai at the matching Camel version. The existing starter continues configuring the component and TypeSafe AI language; register the Semantic expert explicitly as a named Spring bean. Applications using only the component or TypeSafe AI language need no new dependency.

Replace org.apache.camel:camel-wolf-defender with org.apache.camel:camel-semantic-wolf-defender. The Wolf Defender Java packages, classes, model provisioning and configuration properties are unchanged. Its documentation page is now semantic-wolf-defender.adoc.

Update Maven dependencies and Camel JBang --dep options to the new expert artifact names. Each Semantic evaluation still selects an explicit named registry expert. Provider identities and operation names remain typesafe-ai and wolf-defender; only the reported adapter artifact changes.

TypeSafe AI now rejects explicit media parts nested in structured input, instructions or criteria, which previously could be serialized and sent to the provider. Text and JSON-compatible Map/List state remain supported. Binary objects and image, audio or video parts are rejected before HTTP; plain text and ordinary application field names do not imply media. Records such as {type: image, url: …​, sku: 42} and MIME metadata with a URL alone remain application state. Direct Semantic adapter calls and batches use the same validation. Wolf Defender remains text-only with its existing limits.

camel-semantic - explicit experts

Every evaluation in the preview semantic API now requires an explicit expert registry bean name. Move block-level expert values onto each evaluation in YAML and XML; in Java, move .expert(…​) from SemanticEvaluationsBuilder to each SemanticEvaluationBuilder.

The camel.language.semantic.default-expert and camel.language.semantic.adapter options, SemanticLanguage.setDefaultExpert / setAdapter, automatic selection of a sole expert, and META-INF/services/org/apache/camel/semantic-adapter discovery are removed. Register an expert bean explicitly, including its initialization and destruction methods where needed, and reference it from each evaluation. Expert references accept property placeholders, but do not fall back to class loading. The default state selector is unchanged.

The semantic metadata response no longer includes defaultExpert, defaultError or defaultErrorCode. Operation inspection and completion require an explicit expert reference.

camel-semantic - evaluation-only auditing

The preview semantic audit API no longer records route actions. SemanticAuditDecision, SemanticAudit.get(context).decision(…​) and SemanticObserver.decision(…​) have been removed. Remove the corresponding decision beans and processor calls from routes. Evaluation auditing is automatic under the existing audit configuration; normal application routing remains unchanged. Previously persisted version-1 decision records remain readable and exportable as legacy data. Semantic audit browsing no longer accepts route-action, namespace or application-correlation filters. The console’s target filter is also removed. Newly captured evaluation records use definition instead of target; update consumers and queries to read definition. Previously persisted records retain their original fields. Event-ID detail responses no longer expand a decision’s evidence references automatically; use caseEventId for a bounded associated case or eventIds for explicit bulk retrieval. The original persisted record remains available in record. Use automatic evaluation provenance, including entry/executing route and Camel breadcrumb identifiers. The internal CamelSemanticAuditReferences exchange property is no longer populated. In audit health responses, capturing replaces decisionsEnabled; it indicates that some expert enables evaluation capture. For external readers, retained is unknown instead of a count from the unrelated memory store.

SemanticAuditReader.getAll no longer imposes a 100-ID evidence limit. Callers must bound requests and responses; backend implementations may split retrieval into their own batches. The 100-reference limit on legacy persisted evidence records remains unchanged.

Vert.x 5

Camel now uses Vert.x 5. This affects camel-vertx, camel-vertx-http, camel-vertx-websocket, camel-platform-http-vertx, camel-platform-http-main, camel-platform-http-jolokia, camel-knative-http and camel-oauth. Application code that uses the Vert.x API directly, for example to create a custom Vertx instance, a custom Router handler, or an authentication provider for platform-http, must be migrated as described in the Vert.x 5 migration guide. Notably, the callback variants of asynchronous methods are removed in favour of the methods returning a Future.

A metrics or tracing factory can no longer be set on the VertxOptions given to a component (the MetricsOptions.setFactory and TracingOptions.setFactory methods are removed). Either create the Vertx instance with Vertx.builder() and set it on the component (or bind it to the registry), or register the factory as a io.vertx.core.spi.VertxServiceProvider service.

VertxPlatformHttpRouter.mountSubRouter is removed, as it is no longer part of the Vert.x Router API. Use router.route(mountPoint + "*").subRouter(subRouter) instead.

When the connection of a WebSocket is lost without a close frame, the camel-vertx-websocket consumer no longer passes the resulting exception to the consumer exception handler, and no longer routes it as an ERROR event. It is handled as a WebSocket closed with a close frame: a consumer with fireWebSocketConnectionEvents=true routes a CLOSE event, and a consumer with consumeAsClient=true reconnects. With the default fireWebSocketConnectionEvents=false, the lost connection of a peer is therefore no longer logged as a warning.

When the WebSocket of a consumer with consumeAsClient=true reports any other error, the consumer exception handler now receives that exception. Previously it received the cause of that exception, which could be null.

When the WebSocket that the camel-vertx-websocket producer sends to (a server not managed by Camel) reports an error other than a lost connection, the exchange now fails with that exception. Previously it failed with the cause of that exception, which could be null.

camel-arangodb still uses Vert.x 4, as the ArangoDB Java driver does not support Vert.x 5 yet (see arangodb-java-driver#625). An application that uses camel-arangodb together with one of the components above resolves a single version of the io.vertx artifacts, so either the ArangoDB driver or the other components fail at runtime. The workaround suggested by the driver maintainers is to replace com.arangodb:arangodb-java-driver with com.arangodb:arangodb-java-driver-shaded, which embeds its own relocated copy of Vert.x. The vertx option of camel-arangodb cannot be used in that case.

camel-kubernetes - Kubernetes client 8

camel-kubernetes now uses the fabric8 Kubernetes client 8.0, which brings these changes for applications:

  • The client uses its Vert.x 5 HTTP client (io.fabric8:kubernetes-httpclient-vertx-5) instead of the Vert.x 4 one (io.fabric8:kubernetes-httpclient-vertx). An application that excludes the default HTTP client, for example to use OkHttp or the JDK HTTP client, must now exclude kubernetes-httpclient-vertx-5.

  • The client and its model use Jackson 3 (tools.jackson). Jackson 2 does not apply the serializers of the model, so Kubernetes model objects serialized with Jackson 2 come out in a different shape. For example, a targetPort of 8080 is written as {"value":8080,"intVal":8080}, and a CPU limit of 500m as {"amount":"500","format":"m"}. This affects routes that marshal the result of a camel-kubernetes producer with the camel-jackson data format (marshal().json()), and application code that uses its own Jackson 2 ObjectMapper. Replace the camel-jackson dependency with camel-jackson3, which provides the same jackson data format on Jackson 3 (see JSON Jackson 3), so the route does not change. In Java code, use Jackson 3 or io.fabric8.kubernetes.client.utils.Serialization.

  • Jackson 3 uses the Jackson 2 com.fasterxml.jackson.core:jackson-annotations artifact, and needs version 2.21 or later. An application that pins Jackson 2 to 2.20 or older, for example through an older Spring Boot BOM, fails on the first use of the client with NoClassDefFoundError: com/fasterxml/jackson/annotation/JsonSerializeAs. Use jackson-annotations 2.21 or later; Camel uses 2.22.

See the Kubernetes client 8 migration guide for the other changes.

camel-kubernetes - the namespace option, custom resources and scale

  • The producers of the namespaced kubernetes-* components (config-maps, cronjob, deployments, events, hpa, job, persistent-volumes-claims, pods, replication-controllers, resources-quota, secrets, service-accounts and services) use the namespace endpoint option when the CamelKubernetesNamespaceName header is not set; the header still wins. Before, these producers ignored the option: the get, create, update and delete operations failed without the header, and the list operations listed all namespaces. A route that sets the namespace option on a producer endpoint and lists without the header now lists that namespace only.

  • The kubernetes-custom-resources producer uses the crdName, crdGroup, crdScope, crdVersion and crdPlural endpoint options. Before, it checked them but then built the custom resource definition from the headers only.

  • The kubernetes-custom-resources producer takes the namespace from the CamelKubernetesNamespaceName header, or else from the namespace endpoint option, like the other producers. Before, the option won over the header.

  • createCustomResource and updateCustomResource fail the exchange with the KubernetesClientException of a 409 Conflict (the instance already exists, or the update is based on an old resource version). Before, the conflict was logged at INFO and the body was an empty GenericKubernetesResource.

  • scaleDeployment and scaleReplicationController return the replicas of the spec of the scaled resource, the number that was asked for. Before, they returned the replicas of its status, which lags behind until the pods are started or stopped, and failed with a NullPointerException when the resource had no status yet.

  • createNode and updateNode of kubernetes-nodes take the labels of the node from the CamelKubernetesNodesLabels header, the header that listNodesByLabels uses. Before, they read the CamelKubernetesPodsLabels header; a route that sets that header for a node must set CamelKubernetesNodesLabels instead.

  • The openshift-builds, openshift-build-configs and openshift-deploymentconfigs producers take the namespace from the CamelKubernetesNamespaceName header, or else from the namespace endpoint option, in every operation. Before, they ignored the option (used by the openshift-deploymentconfigs consumer only), listBuilds and listBuildConfigs listed all namespaces even when a namespace was set, and listDeploymentConfigs, listDeploymentConfigsByLabels and getDeploymentConfig always used the namespace of the Kubernetes client. Without a namespace, listBuilds and listBuildConfigs still list all namespaces, and the deployment config list and get operations still use the namespace of the client.

  • listBuildsByLabels, listBuildConfigsByLabels and listDeploymentConfigsByLabels without the labels header fail with an IllegalArgumentException that says the labels are required, instead of a NullPointerException.

  • scaleDeploymentConfig returns the replicas of the spec of the scaled deployment config, the number that was asked for. Before, it returned the replicas of its status, which lags behind until the pods are started or stopped, and failed with a NullPointerException when the deployment config had no status yet.

camel-langchain4j-embeddingstore - auto-embedding a TextSegment, a List, a Map or a POJO body, and the REMOVE id

With an embeddingModel and no CamelLangChain4jEmbeddingsEmbedding header, ADD and SEARCH now embed a TextSegment body as is, with its metadata. Before, they embedded and stored its toString().

They now fail the exchange with an InvalidPayloadException for a body that is not text, bytes, a stream, a file or a TextSegment, such as a Map or a POJO. Before, such a body was embedded and stored as its toString(), for example {text=…​}. Convert the body to text before the endpoint, with convertBodyTo(String.class) or a marshal to JSON.

ADD now embeds a List of String or TextSegment as a batch, one entry per element, and returns the List of the IDs. Before, the whole List was embedded and stored as one text (such as [a, b]), and the body was one ID. A List with another element type fails. SEARCH fails on a List body.

REMOVE now fails when the body is not text (a String, bytes or a stream) or a Collection of String IDs. Before, a Map or a number body removed the ID made of its toString(), which usually removed nothing.

camel-jbang - camel run uses MariaDB Connector/J 4

Camel is upgraded to MariaDB Connector/J 4.0 (org.mariadb.jdbc:mariadb-java-client), which is the version camel run downloads for a route or application that uses the MariaDB JDBC driver. The driver has these breaking changes:

  • TLS is enabled by default: sslMode defaults to verify-full. A server without TLS, or with a certificate the client does not trust (such as a self-signed one), now fails the connection. Configure the trust store, or set the sslMode option in the JDBC URL, for example sslMode=trust for a test database with a self-signed certificate, or sslMode=allow.

  • PreparedStatement.executeQuery() rejects a statement that returns no result set. The permitNoResults=true option in the JDBC URL restores the former behavior.

  • setSavepoint() throws in auto-commit mode.

  • AWS IAM authentication moved to the separate org.mariadb.jdbc:mariadb-java-client-aws artifact, which an application using it must now add.

  • Java 17 is the minimum, and the useReadAheadInput option is removed.

See the MariaDB Connector/J changelog for the other changes.

camel-resilience4j, camel-microprofile-fault-tolerance - a circuit breaker in a doTry

A circuit breaker in the steps of a doTry no longer ends that doTry for the steps after it. Before, when the circuit breaker was done, a later routing slip, dynamic router, multicast or recipient list of the same doTry used the error handler of the route (redelivery and the dead letter channel) instead of the doCatch. And after the fallback of the circuit breaker had recovered from a failure, no doCatch of that doTry caught a later failure: the failure was thrown from the doTry. Now such failures go to the doCatch, as for the other steps of the doTry. A failure of the fallback itself is now also caught by the doCatch (before, it was thrown from the doTry).

To have those steps use the error handler of the route, move them out of the doTry block.

camel-aws2-kinesis - each endpoint uses its own client

Each aws2-kinesis endpoint now uses the Kinesis client of its own configuration (region, credentials, uriEndpointOverride, or amazonKinesisClient / amazonKinesisAsyncClient). Before, all endpoints of the component used the client of the endpoint that started first, and stopping one endpoint (for example by removing its route) closed that client for every other endpoint.

Camel now closes only the clients it created. A client set with amazonKinesisClient or amazonKinesisAsyncClient, on the component or on an endpoint, is no longer closed when Camel stops: close it yourself when you no longer need it.

camel-aws2-kinesis - failed records of the KCL consumer

With useKclConsumers=true, a record whose exchange fails (the route ends with an exception) is now reported to the exception handler of the consumer, as the other consumers do. It is still checkpointed with its batch, so it is not consumed again: handle it in the route, for example with a dead letter channel.

The new option kclStopCheckpointOnFailure (default false) keeps the checkpoint of a shard before the first failed record instead, so the next holder of the lease of the shard consumes the records from that one on again. See the component documentation for its cost: the records after the failed one can be routed twice.

camel-aws2-kinesis - the batch producer sends the failed records again and splits at 10 MiB

When the body is an Iterable, the producer now splits the batch into PutRecords requests of at most 500 records and 10 MiB (the data and partition keys of the records). Before, only the number of records was limited, and a request above 10 MiB was rejected by Kinesis. A record above 10 MiB now fails the exchange with an IllegalArgumentException before any request is sent.

When some records of a request are not written, the producer now sends only those records again, up to 3 times with a delay of 100 to 400 milliseconds (plus a random part of up to a quarter of it), and then sends the next requests of the batch.
